So yeah, I don't know if what I'm shaving actually makes a difference to the panic.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of the straight razor would decrease if you were using it in a situation where there was no possibility of harming anyone?\n\nPatient: I don't know, honestly. Like if I was alone in a room with it? I guess... I mean, I'd still be holding it, right? And I'd still be the one holding it, so I could still slip and cut myself. My hands get so sweaty when I even think about it. I have to use that talcum powder we have at the shop just to dry them off sometimes. We use this specific brand, Clubman Pinaud. It has that classic barbershop smell, you know? Sort of powdery and masculine. My dad used to wear a cologne that smelled just like it. Old Spice, I think. Or maybe Brut? He always got those gift sets for Christmas with the aftershave and the deodorant. I still buy him one every year, just out of habit. He's retired now, lives down in Florida. Plays golf every single day. I don't get the appeal of golf, honestly. It just seems like a lot of walking in the hot sun to hit a tiny ball. I'd rather be fishing. We used to go fishing on this lake when I was a teenager. Caught a massive largemouth bass once, must have been five pounds. \n\nBut, uh, what were you asking? Oh, if nobody could get hurt. I don't know, I just don't think there's any situation where nobody could get hurt if I'm holding an open razor. I'm there, so I could get hurt. It's just the razor itself, I think.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of the straight razor be less intense if you were to use it for shaving a mannequin or a dummy, rather than a real person?\n\nPatient: A mannequin? Man, I haven't seen one of those since barber college either. We had these heads we called \"pivot points,\" they had this weirdly realistic hair that you could actually cut and style. Some guys would get so attached to their mannequins, they'd name them and talk to them while they were cutting. There was this one guy, Tommy, he named his \"Brenda\" and he'd actually get mad if someone else touched her. Tommy was a trip. He ended up dropping out and moving to Vegas to become a dealer at one of the casinos. Blackjack, I think. I went to Vegas once for my 21st birthday. We stayed at the Luxor, you know, the giant pyramid? It was so confusing inside, the elevators go up at an angle. We got so lost trying to find our room the first night. We ended up at this buffet at like 3 AM eating prime rib and crab legs. The crab legs were okay, but I've had way better in Maryland. My aunt lives in Annapolis, and she makes this crab dip that is just unbelievable. She puts Old Bay seasoning in everything. She even puts it on popcorn, which sounds gross, but it's actually really good if you have a cold beer with it. \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, the dummy. I don't know, honestly. Probably not. Because, like I was saying, it's just the razor itself. It doesn't really matter what's in front of me, real person or plastic head. If I'm holding that blade open, I'm just terrified I'm going to slip and slice my own hand open or something. So no, I don't think it would be any less intense.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your fear of the straight razor would decrease if someone else was holding it, but you were still in the room?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 27 / barber / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
If someone else was holding it... yeah, I think so. If I'm not the one holding it, it's not as bad. Like, the other guys at the shop use them all the time. My buddy Marcus, he's in the chair next to mine, he does these incredibly sharp line-ups with his. He's got this custom-made handle, it's like a dark walnut wood with his initials engraved on it. He paid a fortune for it from this guy on Instagram who makes them by hand. Instagram is crazy for stuff like that now. You can find people making anything. I follow this one account where a guy just restores old axes. He finds them rusted in barns and polishes them up until they look brand new. It's weirdly relaxing to watch. He uses this special grinding wheel, and the sparks fly everywhere. Reminds me of my high school shop class. We had to make a birdhouse, which was so cliché, but I actually tried to make mine look like a miniature log cabin. I spent weeks cutting little dowels for the logs. A squirrel ended up moving into it instead of a bird, though. It chewed the hole bigger and just took over. Squirrels are relentless, man. They get into the bird feeders at my parents' house, and my dad has tried every trick in the book to keep them out. He even put hot sauce on the seeds once, but I think they actually liked it. 

But yeah, anyway, sorry. If Marcus is holding his razor, or anyone else is, I'm fine. I can stand right next to him and talk to him while he's using it. It's just... it's just when it's in *my* hand that I feel like I'm gonna lose control. If I don't have to touch it, the panic isn't really there.

A good hot towel shave usually takes about, what, fifteen minutes? She spent like two hours looking at these vintage Pyrex bowls. The ones with the little flowers on the side, you know? Apparently, they're super collectible now. Some of them go for hundreds of dollars online, which is crazy to me because my grandma used to have the exact same ones and she just kept leftover meatloaf in them. My grandma made the best meatloaf, too. She put a little bit of brown sugar in the ketchup glaze on top. It sounds weird, but it caramelized in the oven and it was incredible. She used to serve it with these mashed potatoes that had way too much butter in them, but nobody complained. \n\nBut, uh, no, the razor thing doesn't really bother me at home or anything. Just at the shop when I have to use it.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social situations or activities outside of work because of your fear of straight razors?\n\nPatient: No, no, I still go out and do stuff. Like I said, I don't really run into straight razors at a bar or a movie theater, right? So it doesn't stop me from seeing my friends or my girlfriend. Actually, me and my buddies are supposed to go to this axe-throwing place this weekend. Have you ever done that? It's wild. You stand in these little cages and just chuck a hatchet at a wooden target. First time I went, I was terrible at it. The axe kept bouncing off the wood and flying back at me. The instructor said it was all in the wrist, like skipping a rock. I used to be so good at skipping rocks when I was a kid. We lived near this lake, Lake Winnebago, and my dad would take me out there on Sunday mornings. The water was always so still, like glass. He taught me how to find the perfect flat stone. You want one that's smooth, but not too heavy. My dad had this crazy record, he could get a rock to skip like twelve times. I think my best was maybe seven. He always wore this ridiculous bucket hat when we went to the lake, covered in fishing lures. He thought it was hilarious, but my mom hated it. She actually tried to throw it away once, but he fished it out of the trash... \n\nBut yeah, anyway, social stuff is fine. Well, I guess you could cut stuff with it, like opening boxes or something. Speaking of boxes, we got this massive shipment of new pomades at the shop yesterday. The boxes were huge, and they were packed with all these little styrofoam peanuts. I hate those things. They stick to everything because of the static electricity. You try to throw them away and they just cling to your hand, your shirt, the trash can... it's a nightmare. We had to spend twenty minutes just sweeping up all the packing peanuts before we could even unpack the product. The pomade is pretty nice, though. It's got this sort of matte finish, smells like sandalwood and... maybe a little bit of bergamot? My old roommate used to burn this sandalwood incense all the time. He was super into meditation and yoga and all that. He had this whole shrine set up in the corner of the living room with crystals and singing bowls. He tried to teach me how to meditate once, but I just couldn't clear my mind, you know? I kept thinking about what I was gonna have for dinner. I think we ordered Thai food that night. Pad Thai, extra spicy... \n\nBut, uh, to answer your question, I don't know if it's just the shaving part. I think it's just the razor itself. Even if I was just using it to open one of those boxes I was talking about, it wouldn't matter. It's just... I know it’s just a tool, you know? I went to school for this, I trained with it, and I can use clippers and shears all day long without batting an eye. But when that straight razor is just sitting there, open at my station... man, it just feels like I’m about to hurt somebody, or like I'm gonna slip and cut myself really badly. Anyway, my buddy in class, Steve, he accidentally cut off a chunk of his own thumb while texturizing the mannequin's hair. Bled everywhere. He was this old guy with a huge white beard, looked exactly like Santa Claus in overalls. He had a tractor that he let us sit on once. It was a John Deere, bright green. I always thought tractors were so cool when I was a kid. I wanted to be a farmer for like a year, until I realized how early they have to wake up. I am definitely not a morning person. Even now, getting to the barbershop by 8 AM is a struggle. I usually need at least two cups of coffee before I can even hold a conversation. \n\nBut, um, what were we talking about? Oh, the blunt razor. Yeah, I don't know. We used to go through so many balloons. Just those cheap ones from the dollar store. My instructor, Mr. Davis, he'd blow them up so tight that if you even breathed wrong with the blade, they'd pop and shaving cream would go everywhere. I actually hate the smell of that cheap foam shaving cream now. It reminds me of my grandpa, though. He used to use it every morning. He had this little badger hair brush and a ceramic mug. He was a mechanic, so his hands were always covered in grease, but his face was always perfectly smooth. He worked on airplanes, actually. Commercial jets. We got to go to the hangar once when I was like ten. It's crazy how massive those planes are up close. You don't realize it when you're sitting inside them, eating those tiny bags of pretzels. I always ask for the Biscoff cookies instead, those things are amazing with coffee. I tried to buy a box of them at the grocery store once, but they just didn't taste the same on the ground. Maybe it's the altitude? I read somewhere that your taste buds get numb when you're flying. \n\nBut, um... wait, the balloon thing. Honestly, I'm not really sure if it would change anything. I don't think it would. Because I'd still be holding the razor, and it would still be open in my hand. So yeah, I don't know if what I'm shaving actually makes a difference to the panic.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of the straight razor would decrease if you were using it in a situation where there was no possibility of harming anyone?\n\nPatient: I don't know, honestly. But when that straight razor is just sitting open at my station, I just... I get this overwhelming feeling that I’m about to hurt somebody. Or like I'm going to slip and cut myself really badly. It's just sitting there, and I can't stop thinking about the blade.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of harming someone or making a mistake limited to when you're using the straight razor, or do you worry about it with other tools or situations at work as well?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just the straight razor. I mean, I use scissors all day, clippers, trimmers... none of that bothers me. I can do a skin fade in my sleep. Actually, I had this guy come in last week who wanted this super intricate design shaved into the side of his head—like a geometric pattern thing. It took me almost an hour just with the trimmers. He was a graphic designer or something, worked for this start-up downtown that makes an app for... I don't even know, dog walking? He showed me the app, it had this little cartoon corgi as the logo. My sister used to have a corgi, named him Waffles. Cutest dog ever, but he shed everywhere. You'd find dog hair in your food, in your shoes... she ended up having to buy this crazy expensive vacuum cleaner just to keep up with it. One of those ones that looks like a spaceship, you know? \n\nBut yeah, no, I don't worry about hurting anyone with the scissors or the clippers. It's literally just the straight razor. When it's just laying there open, that's when my brain goes to that dark place.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of using the straight razor affecting other parts of your life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? Um... no, not really. I mean, I don't really encounter straight razors anywhere else, you know? Unless I'm at an antique store or something, but I don't really go to those. My girlfriend loves them, though. We went to this huge antique mall last weekend, the kind that’s in an old converted barn? It smelled like dust and old books. She spent like two hours looking at these vintage Pyrex bowls. The ones with the little flowers on the side, you know? Apparently, they're super collectible now. Some of them go for hundreds of dollars online, which is crazy to me because my grandma used to have the exact same ones and she just kept leftover meatloaf in them. My grandma made the best meatloaf, too. She put a little bit of brown sugar in the ketchup glaze on top. It sounds weird, but it caramelized in the oven and it was incredible. She used to serve it with these mashed potatoes that had way too much butter in them, but nobody complained. \n\nBut, uh, no, the razor thing doesn't really bother me at home or anything. Just at the shop when I have to use it.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social situations or activities outside of work because of your fear of straight razors?\n\nPatient: No, no, I still go out and do stuff. Like I said, I don't really run into straight razors at a bar or a movie theater, right? So it doesn't stop me from seeing my friends or my girlfriend. Actually, me and my buddies are supposed to go to this axe-throwing place this weekend. Have you ever done that? It's wild. You stand in these little cages and just chuck a hatchet at a wooden target. First time I went, I was terrible at it. The axe kept bouncing off the wood and flying back at me. The instructor said it was all in the wrist, like skipping a rock. I used to be so good at skipping rocks when I was a kid. We lived near this lake, Lake Winnebago, and my dad would take me out there on Sunday mornings. The water was always so still, like glass. He taught me how to find the perfect flat stone. You want one that's smooth, but not too heavy. My dad had this crazy record, he could get a rock to skip like twelve times. I think my best was maybe seven. He always wore this ridiculous bucket hat when we went to the lake, covered in fishing lures. He thought it was hilarious, but my mom hated it. She actually tried to throw it away once, but he fished it out of the trash... \n\nBut yeah, anyway, social stuff is fine. I'm not avoiding going out or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of the straight razor related to the fact that it's used for shaving, or would you be just as afraid of it if you were using it for something else?\n\nPatient: Um... I'm not really sure, honestly. I've never really thought about using it for anything else. I mean, it's a straight razor, so shaving is pretty much all it's good for, right? Well, I guess you could cut stuff with it, like opening boxes or something. Speaking of boxes, we got this massive shipment of new pomades at the shop yesterday. The boxes were huge, and they were packed with all these little styrofoam peanuts. I hate those things. They stick to everything because of the static electricity. You try to throw them away and they just cling to your hand, your shirt, the trash can... it's a nightmare. We had to spend twenty minutes just sweeping up all the packing peanuts before we could even unpack the product. The pomade is pretty nice, though. It's got this sort of matte finish, smells like sandalwood and... maybe a little bit of bergamot? My old roommate used to burn this sandalwood incense all the time. He was super into meditation and yoga and all that. He had this whole shrine set up in the corner of the living room with crystals and singing bowls. He tried to teach me how to meditate once, but I just couldn't clear my mind, you know? I kept thinking about what I was gonna have for dinner. I think we ordered Thai food that night. Pad Thai, extra spicy... \n\nBut, uh, to answer your question, I don't know if it's just the shaving part. I think it's just the razor itself. Just the way it looks when it's open.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel afraid of the straight razor if you were using it for a task that didn't involve shaving?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, I think so. Even if I was just using it to open one of those boxes I was talking about, it wouldn't matter. It's just... I know it’s just a tool, you know? I went to school for this, I trained with it, and I can use clippers and shears all day long without batting an eye. But when that straight razor is just sitting there, open at my station... man, it just feels like I’m about to hurt somebody, or like I'm gonna slip and cut myself really badly. It's crazy because I've never even nicked anyone before. Well, except this one time in barber college, but that was with scissors, not a razor. We were practicing on these mannequin heads first, right? They had real human hair, which was always a little creepy to me. I always wondered where they got the hair from. Like, do people donate it specifically for mannequins, or is it just leftover from wig makers? Anyway, my buddy in class, Steve, he accidentally cut off a chunk of his own thumb while texturizing the mannequin's hair. Bled everywhere. The instructor had to wrap it up in this huge bandage, and Steve couldn't cut hair for a week. He ended up dropping out a month later to go work at his uncle's auto body shop. I saw him a couple of years ago at a gas station, he looked good. He was driving this vintage Mustang that he restored himself. Cherry red, white leather interior... beautiful car. I've always wanted a classic car, maybe a '69 Charger. \n\nBut yeah, anyway, it doesn't matter what I'm using the razor for. Just seeing it open like that, it just... it terrifies me that someone's gonna get hurt.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be afraid of the straight razor if it were blunt and couldn't cut anyone?\n\nPatient: I don't know, maybe? If it was completely blunt and couldn't cut anything... I guess it wouldn't be as bad, but it would still look the same, you know? Just that shape of it. It's funny you mention blunt things, because my mom used to have these butter knives that were so old and worn down they couldn't even cut hot butter. We had this big Sunday breakfast tradition at my house growing up. Pancakes, bacon, the whole nine yards. My dad made the best pancakes. He'd put chocolate chips in them, but only on my brother's side of the griddle, because I hated chocolate chips in my pancakes. I liked blueberries. Fresh ones, not the frozen kind that turn the whole pancake purple. We used to go berry picking every summer at this farm upstate. You'd get these little green baskets and just fill them up. My brother would eat half of what he picked before we even got to the weigh station. The farmer always knew, but he never said anything. He was this old guy with a huge white beard, looked exactly like Santa Claus in overalls. He had a tractor that he let us sit on once. It was a John Deere, bright green. I always thought tractors were so cool when I was a kid. I wanted to be a farmer for like a year, until I realized how early they have to wake up. I am definitely not a morning person. Even now, getting to the barbershop by 8 AM is a struggle. I usually need at least two cups of coffee before I can even hold a conversation. \n\nBut, um, what were we talking about? Oh, the blunt razor. Yeah, I don't know. I think just seeing it open would still give me that panic, even if I knew it was dull.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of the straight razor change if you were using it for a task that is very similar to shaving but not actually shaving, such as shaving cream off a balloon like you did in training?\n\nPatient: Shaving a balloon... man, I haven't thought about that since barber college. We used to go through so many balloons. Just those cheap ones from the dollar store. My instructor, Mr. Davis, he'd blow them up so tight that if you even breathed wrong with the blade, they'd pop and shaving cream would go everywhere. I actually hate the smell of that cheap foam shaving cream now. It reminds me of my grandpa, though. He used to use it every morning. He had this little badger hair brush and a ceramic mug. He was a mechanic, so his hands were always covered in grease, but his face was always perfectly smooth. He worked on airplanes, actually. Commercial jets. We got to go to the hangar once when I was like ten. It's crazy how massive those planes are up close. You don't realize it when you're sitting inside them, eating those tiny bags of pretzels. I always ask for the Biscoff cookies instead, those things are amazing with coffee. I tried to buy a box of them at the grocery store once, but they just didn't taste the same on the ground. Maybe it's the altitude? I read somewhere that your taste buds get numb when you're flying. \n\nBut, um... wait, the balloon thing. Honestly, I'm not really sure if it would change anything. I don't think it would. Because I'd still be holding the razor, and it would still be open in my hand. So yeah, I don't know if what I'm shaving actually makes a difference to the panic.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of the straight razor would decrease if you were using it in a situation where there was no possibility of harming anyone?\n\nPatient: I don't know, honestly. Like if I was alone in a room with it? I guess... I mean, I'd still be holding it, right? And I'd still be the one holding it, so I could still slip and cut myself. My hands get so sweaty when I even think about it. I have to use that talcum powder we have at the shop just to dry them off sometimes. We use this specific brand, Clubman Pinaud. It has that classic barbershop smell, you know? Sort of powdery and masculine. My dad used to wear a cologne that smelled just like it. Old Spice, I think. Or maybe Brut? He always got those gift sets for Christmas with the aftershave and the deodorant. I still buy him one every year, just out of habit. He's retired now, lives down in Florida. Plays golf every single day. I don't get the appeal of golf, honestly. It just seems like a lot of walking in the hot sun to hit a tiny ball. I'd rather be fishing. We used to go fishing on this lake when I was a teenager. Caught a massive largemouth bass once, must have been five pounds. \n\nBut, uh, what were you asking? Oh, if nobody could get hurt. I don't know, I just don't think there's any situation where nobody could get hurt if I'm holding an open razor. I'm there, so I could get hurt. It's just the razor itself, I think.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of the straight razor be less intense if you were to use it for shaving a mannequin or a dummy, rather than a real person?\n\nPatient: A mannequin? Man, I haven't seen one of those since barber college either. We had these heads we called \"pivot points,\" they had this weirdly realistic hair that you could actually cut and style. Some guys would get so attached to their mannequins, they'd name them and talk to them while they were cutting. There was this one guy, Tommy, he named his \"Brenda\" and he'd actually get mad if someone else touched her. Tommy was a trip. He ended up dropping out and moving to Vegas to become a dealer at one of the casinos. Blackjack, I think. I went to Vegas once for my 21st birthday. We stayed at the Luxor, you know, the giant pyramid? It was so confusing inside, the elevators go up at an angle. We got so lost trying to find our room the first night. We ended up at this buffet at like 3 AM eating prime rib and crab legs. The crab legs were okay, but I've had way better in Maryland. My aunt lives in Annapolis, and she makes this crab dip that is just unbelievable. She puts Old Bay seasoning in everything. She even puts it on popcorn, which sounds gross, but it's actually really good if you have a cold beer with it. \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, the dummy. I don't know, honestly. Probably not. Because, like I was saying, it's just the razor itself. It doesn't really matter what's in front of me, real person or plastic head. If I'm holding that blade open, I'm just terrified I'm going to slip and slice my own hand open or something. So no, I don't think it would be any less intense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of the straight razor would decrease if someone else was holding it, but you were still in the room?\n\nPatient: If someone else was holding it... yeah, I think so. If I'm not the one holding it, it's not as bad. Like, the other guys at the shop use them all the time. My buddy Marcus, he's in the chair next to mine, he does these incredibly sharp line-ups with his. He's got this custom-made handle, it's like a dark walnut wood with his initials engraved on it. He paid a fortune for it from this guy on Instagram who makes them by hand. Instagram is crazy for stuff like that now. You can find people making anything. I follow this one account where a guy just restores old axes. He finds them rusted in barns and polishes them up until they look brand new. It's weirdly relaxing to watch. He uses this special grinding wheel, and the sparks fly everywhere. Reminds me of my high school shop class. We had to make a birdhouse, which was so cliché, but I actually tried to make mine look like a miniature log cabin. I spent weeks cutting little dowels for the logs. A squirrel ended up moving into it instead of a bird, though. It chewed the hole bigger and just took over. Squirrels are relentless, man. They get into the bird feeders at my parents' house, and my dad has tried every trick in the book to keep them out. He even put hot sauce on the seeds once, but I think they actually liked it. \n\nBut yeah, anyway, sorry. If Marcus is holding his razor, or anyone else is, I'm fine. I can stand right next to him and talk to him while he's using it. It's just... it's just when it's in *my* hand that I feel like I'm gonna lose control. If I don't have to touch it, the panic isn't really there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, losing control... I think that's exactly it. I mean, I know it’s just a tool I trained with. I can use my clippers all day long without even thinking about it. I actually just upgraded to these new cordless Wahl clippers, and the battery life on them is unbelievable. I used to have the corded ones, and the thick cord would always get tangled around the hydraulic pump on the barber chair. One time, it got so wrapped up I actually tripped and almost took my client down with me. He was a really good sport about it, thankfully. He’s this guy who owns a bakery down the street, and he always brings in these amazing cheese danishes when he comes in for a fade. I don't even have much of a sweet tooth, usually. I'm more of a savory guy, you know? Give me a good steak over a piece of cake any day. But man, those danishes. They remind me of how my mom used to bake when I was a kid. She made this incredible banana bread with chocolate chips in it. We had this massive walnut tree in our backyard, and we'd spend hours in the fall just cracking walnuts on the patio to put in the bread. My hands would get stained completely brown from the husks. It took days to scrub it off. 

But, uh, anyway. Sorry. What were we talking about? Oh, the razor. Yeah, like I was saying, I can use clippers all day, but when that straight razor is just sitting open at my station... it feels like I’m about to hurt somebody or cut myself badly. Just having it sitting there open makes me feel like something terrible is guaranteed to happen.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hey, yeah. Thanks. So, I'm a barber. I actually went to college, got my degree and everything, but I ended up working at this barbershop downtown. Actually, getting them into the shop was a total nightmare, my buddy threw his back out just trying to get them over the curb... but anyway, I'm here because... well, I’ve gotten to the point where I’ll do almost anything to avoid using the straight razor at work. \n\nI'll ask the other guys to switch clients with me, or I'll just tell the customer the blade’s missing, or I'll literally hide the thing in my drawer. He used to make us practice on balloons covered in shaving cream. If you popped the balloon, you failed for the day. I swear, the sound of a balloon popping in that room sounded like a gunshot. It was so echoey in there. The building was this old converted warehouse, high ceilings, exposed ductwork... freezing in the winter, honestly. We all had to wear these thick wool sweaters under our smocks. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I can use clippers all day long. Buzz cuts, fades, whatever, no problem. But when that straight razor is just sitting open at my station, I just... I get this overwhelming feeling that I’m about to hurt somebody. Or like I'm going to slip and cut myself really badly. It's just sitting there, and I can't stop thinking about the blade.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of harming someone or making a mistake limited to when you're using the straight razor, or do you worry about it with other tools or situations at work as well?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just the straight razor. I mean, I use scissors all day, clippers, trimmers... none of that bothers me. I can do a skin fade in my sleep. Actually, I had this guy come in last week who wanted this super intricate design shaved into the side of his head—like a geometric pattern thing. It took me almost an hour just with the trimmers. He was a graphic designer or something, worked for this start-up downtown that makes an app for... I don't even know, dog walking? He showed me the app, it had this little cartoon corgi as the logo. Um... no, not really. I mean, I don't really encounter straight razors anywhere else, you know? Unless I'm at an antique store or something, but I don't really go to those. My girlfriend loves them, though. We went to this huge antique mall last weekend, the kind that’s in an old converted barn? It smelled like dust and old books. She spent like two hours looking at these vintage Pyrex bowls. The ones with the little flowers on the side, you know? Apparently, they're super collectible now. Some of them go for hundreds of dollars online, which is crazy to me because my grandma used to have the exact same ones and she just kept leftover meatloaf in them. My grandma made the best meatloaf, too. She put a little bit of brown sugar in the ketchup glaze on top. It sounds weird, but it caramelized in the oven and it was incredible. She used to serve it with these mashed potatoes that had way too much butter in them, but nobody complained. \n\nBut, uh, no, the razor thing doesn't really bother me at home or anything. Just at the shop when I have to use it.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social situations or activities outside of work because of your fear of straight razors?\n\nPatient: No, no, I still go out and do stuff. Like I said, I don't really run into straight razors at a bar or a movie theater, right? So it doesn't stop me from seeing my friends or my girlfriend. Actually, me and my buddies are supposed to go to this axe-throwing place this weekend. Have you ever done that? It's wild. You stand in these little cages and just chuck a hatchet at a wooden target. First time I went, I was terrible at it. The axe kept bouncing off the wood and flying back at me. The instructor said it was all in the wrist, like skipping a rock. I used to be so good at skipping rocks when I was a kid. We lived near this lake, Lake Winnebago, and my dad would take me out there on Sunday mornings. The water was always so still, like glass. He taught me how to find the perfect flat stone. You want one that's smooth, but not too heavy. My dad had this crazy record, he could get a rock to skip like twelve times. I think my best was maybe seven. He always wore this ridiculous bucket hat when we went to the lake, covered in fishing lures. He thought it was hilarious, but my mom hated it. She actually tried to throw it away once, but he fished it out of the trash... \n\nBut yeah, anyway, social stuff is fine. I'm not really sure, honestly. I've never really thought about using it for anything else. I mean, it's a straight razor, so shaving is pretty much all it's good for, right? Well, I guess you could cut stuff with it, like opening boxes or something. Speaking of boxes, we got this massive shipment of new pomades at the shop yesterday. The boxes were huge, and they were packed with all these little styrofoam peanuts. I hate those things. They stick to everything because of the static electricity. You try to throw them away and they just cling to your hand, your shirt, the trash can... it's a nightmare. We had to spend twenty minutes just sweeping up all the packing peanuts before we could even unpack the product. The pomade is pretty nice, though. It's got this sort of matte finish, smells like sandalwood and... maybe a little bit of bergamot? My old roommate used to burn this sandalwood incense all the time. He was super into meditation and yoga and all that. He had this whole shrine set up in the corner of the living room with crystals and singing bowls. He tried to teach me how to meditate once, but I just couldn't clear my mind, you know? I kept thinking about what I was gonna have for dinner. I think we ordered Thai food that night. Pad Thai, extra spicy... \n\nBut, uh, to answer your question, I don't know if it's just the shaving part. I think it's just the razor itself. Even if I was just using it to open one of those boxes I was talking about, it wouldn't matter. It's just... I know it’s just a tool, you know? I went to school for this, I trained with it, and I can use clippers and shears all day long without batting an eye. But when that straight razor is just sitting there, open at my station... man, it just feels like I’m about to hurt somebody, or like I'm gonna slip and cut myself really badly. It's crazy because I've never even nicked anyone before. Well, except this one time in barber college, but that was with scissors, not a razor. We were practicing on these mannequin heads first, right? They had real human hair, which was always a little creepy to me. I always wondered where they got the hair from. Like, do people donate it specifically for mannequins, or is it just leftover from wig makers? Anyway, my buddy in class, Steve, he accidentally cut off a chunk of his own thumb while texturizing the mannequin's hair. Bled everywhere. The instructor had to wrap it up in this huge bandage, and Steve couldn't cut hair for a week. He ended up dropping out a month later to go work at his uncle's auto body shop. I saw him a couple of years ago at a gas station, he looked good. He was driving this vintage Mustang that he restored himself. Cherry red, white leather interior... beautiful car. I've always wanted a classic car, maybe a '69 Charger. \n\nBut yeah, anyway, it doesn't matter what I'm using the razor for. Just seeing it open like that, it just... it terrifies me that someone's gonna get hurt.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be afraid of the straight razor if it were blunt and couldn't cut anyone?\n\nPatient: I don't know, maybe? If it was completely blunt and couldn't cut anything... I guess it wouldn't be as bad, but it would still look the same, you know? Just that shape of it. It's funny you mention blunt things, because my mom used to have these butter knives that were so old and worn down they couldn't even cut hot butter. We had this big Sunday breakfast tradition at my house growing up. Pancakes, bacon, the whole nine yards. My dad made the best pancakes. He'd put chocolate chips in them, but only on my brother's side of the griddle, because I hated chocolate chips in my pancakes. I liked blueberries. He was this old guy with a huge white beard, looked exactly like Santa Claus in overalls. He had a tractor that he let us sit on once. It was a John Deere, bright green. I always thought tractors were so cool when I was a kid. I wanted to be a farmer for like a year, until I realized how early they have to wake up. I am definitely not a morning person. Even now, getting to the barbershop by 8 AM is a struggle. I usually need at least two cups of coffee before I can even hold a conversation. \n\nBut, um, what were we talking about? Oh, the blunt razor. Yeah, I don't know. We used to go through so many balloons. Just those cheap ones from the dollar store. My instructor, Mr. Davis, he'd blow them up so tight that if you even breathed wrong with the blade, they'd pop and shaving cream would go everywhere. I actually hate the smell of that cheap foam shaving cream now. It reminds me of my grandpa, though. He used to use it every morning. He had this little badger hair brush and a ceramic mug. He was a mechanic, so his hands were always covered in grease, but his face was always perfectly smooth. He worked on airplanes, actually. Commercial jets. We got to go to the hangar once when I was like ten. It's crazy how massive those planes are up close. You don't realize it when you're sitting inside them, eating those tiny bags of pretzels. I always ask for the Biscoff cookies instead, those things are amazing with coffee. I tried to buy a box of them at the grocery store once, but they just didn't taste the same on the ground. Maybe it's the altitude? I read somewhere that your taste buds get numb when you're flying. \n\nBut, um... wait, the balloon thing. Honestly, I'm not really sure if it would change anything. I don't think it would. Because I'd still be holding the razor, and it would still be open in my hand. So yeah, I don't know if what I'm shaving actually makes a difference to the panic.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of the straight razor would decrease if you were using it in a situation where there was no possibility of harming anyone?\n\nPatient: I don't know, honestly. Like if I was alone in a room with it? I guess... I mean, I'd still be holding it, right? And I'd still be the one holding it, so I could still slip and cut myself. My hands get so sweaty when I even think about it. I have to use that talcum powder we have at the shop just to dry them off sometimes. We use this specific brand, Clubman Pinaud. It has that classic barbershop smell, you know? Sort of powdery and masculine. My dad used to wear a cologne that smelled just like it. Old Spice, I think. Or maybe Brut? He always got those gift sets for Christmas with the aftershave and the deodorant. I still buy him one every year, just out of habit. He's retired now, lives down in Florida. Plays golf every single day. I don't get the appeal of golf, honestly. It just seems like a lot of walking in the hot sun to hit a tiny ball. I'd rather be fishing. We used to go fishing on this lake when I was a teenager. Caught a massive largemouth bass once, must have been five pounds. \n\nBut, uh, what were you asking? Oh, if nobody could get hurt. I don't know, I just don't think there's any situation where nobody could get hurt if I'm holding an open razor. I'm there, so I could get hurt. It's just the razor itself, I think.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of the straight razor be less intense if you were to use it for shaving a mannequin or a dummy, rather than a real person?\n\nPatient: A mannequin? Man, I haven't seen one of those since barber college either. We had these heads we called \"pivot points,\" they had this weirdly realistic hair that you could actually cut and style. Some guys would get so attached to their mannequins, they'd name them and talk to them while they were cutting. There was this one guy, Tommy, he named his \"Brenda\" and he'd actually get mad if someone else touched her. Tommy was a trip. He ended up dropping out and moving to Vegas to become a dealer at one of the casinos. Blackjack, I think. I went to Vegas once for my 21st birthday. We stayed at the Luxor, you know, the giant pyramid? It was so confusing inside, the elevators go up at an angle. We got so lost trying to find our room the first night. We ended up at this buffet at like 3 AM eating prime rib and crab legs. The crab legs were okay, but I've had way better in Maryland. My aunt lives in Annapolis, and she makes this crab dip that is just unbelievable. She puts Old Bay seasoning in everything. She even puts it on popcorn, which sounds gross, but it's actually really good if you have a cold beer with it. \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, the dummy. I don't know, honestly. Probably not. Because, like I was saying, it's just the razor itself. It doesn't really matter what's in front of me, real person or plastic head. If I'm holding that blade open, I'm just terrified I'm going to slip and slice my own hand open or something. So no, I don't think it would be any less intense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of the straight razor would decrease if someone else was holding it, but you were still in the room?\n\nPatient: If someone else was holding it... yeah, I think so. If I'm not the one holding it, it's not as bad. Like, the other guys at the shop use them all the time. My buddy Marcus, he's in the chair next to mine, he does these incredibly sharp line-ups with his. He's got this custom-made handle, it's like a dark walnut wood with his initials engraved on it. He paid a fortune for it from this guy on Instagram who makes them by hand. Instagram is crazy for stuff like that now. You can find people making anything. I follow this one account where a guy just restores old axes. He finds them rusted in barns and polishes them up until they look brand new. It's weirdly relaxing to watch. He uses this special grinding wheel, and the sparks fly everywhere. Reminds me of my high school shop class. We had to make a birdhouse, which was so cliché, but I actually tried to make mine look like a miniature log cabin. I spent weeks cutting little dowels for the logs. A squirrel ended up moving into it instead of a bird, though. It chewed the hole bigger and just took over. Squirrels are relentless, man. They get into the bird feeders at my parents' house, and my dad has tried every trick in the book to keep them out. He even put hot sauce on the seeds once, but I think they actually liked it. \n\nBut yeah, anyway, sorry. If Marcus is holding his razor, or anyone else is, I'm fine. I can stand right next to him and talk to him while he's using it. It's just... it's just when it's in *my* hand that I feel like I'm gonna lose control. If I don't have to touch it, the panic isn't really there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of the straight razor is because you feel like you might lose control of it when it's in your hand?\n\nPatient: Yeah, losing control... You'd find dog hair in your food, in your shoes... she ended up having to buy this crazy expensive vacuum cleaner just to keep up with it. One of those ones that looks like a spaceship, you know? \n\nBut yeah, no, I don't worry about hurting anyone with the scissors or the clippers. It's literally just the straight razor. When it's just laying there open, that's when my brain goes to that dark place.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of using the straight razor affecting other parts of your life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? Um... no, not really. I mean, I don't really encounter straight razors anywhere else, you know? Unless I'm at an antique store or something, but I don't really go to those. My girlfriend loves them, though. We went to this huge antique mall last weekend, the kind that’s in an old converted barn? It smelled like dust and old books. She spent like two hours looking at these vintage Pyrex bowls. The ones with the little flowers on the side, you know? Apparently, they're super collectible now. Some of them go for hundreds of dollars online, which is crazy to me because my grandma used to have the exact same ones and she just kept leftover meatloaf in them. My grandma made the best meatloaf, too. She put a little bit of brown sugar in the ketchup glaze on top. It sounds weird, but it caramelized in the oven and it was incredible. She used to serve it with these mashed potatoes that had way too much butter in them, but nobody complained. \n\nBut, uh, no, the razor thing doesn't really bother me at home or anything. Just at the shop when I have to use it.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social situations or activities outside of work because of your fear of straight razors?\n\nPatient: No, no, I still go out and do stuff. Like I said, I don't really run into straight razors at a bar or a movie theater, right? So it doesn't stop me from seeing my friends or my girlfriend. Actually, me and my buddies are supposed to go to this axe-throwing place this weekend. Have you ever done that? It's wild. You stand in these little cages and just chuck a hatchet at a wooden target. First time I went, I was terrible at it. The axe kept bouncing off the wood and flying back at me. The instructor said it was all in the wrist, like skipping a rock. I used to be so good at skipping rocks when I was a kid. We lived near this lake, Lake Winnebago, and my dad would take me out there on Sunday mornings. The water was always so still, like glass. He taught me how to find the perfect flat stone. You want one that's smooth, but not too heavy. My dad had this crazy record, he could get a rock to skip like twelve times. I think my best was maybe seven. He always wore this ridiculous bucket hat when we went to the lake, covered in fishing lures. He thought it was hilarious, but my mom hated it. She actually tried to throw it away once, but he fished it out of the trash... \n\nBut yeah, anyway, social stuff is fine. I'm not avoiding going out or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of the straight razor related to the fact that it's used for shaving, or would you be just as afraid of it if you were using it for something else?\n\nPatient: Um... I'm not really sure, honestly. I've never really thought about using it for anything else. I mean, it's a straight razor, so shaving is pretty much all it's good for, right? Well, I guess you could cut stuff with it, like opening boxes or something. Speaking of boxes, we got this massive shipment of new pomades at the shop yesterday. The boxes were huge, and they were packed with all these little styrofoam peanuts. I hate those things. They stick to everything because of the static electricity. You try to throw them away and they just cling to your hand, your shirt, the trash can... it's a nightmare. We had to spend twenty minutes just sweeping up all the packing peanuts before we could even unpack the product. The pomade is pretty nice, though. It's got this sort of matte finish, smells like sandalwood and... maybe a little bit of bergamot? My old roommate used to burn this sandalwood incense all the time. He was super into meditation and yoga and all that. He had this whole shrine set up in the corner of the living room with crystals and singing bowls. He tried to teach me how to meditate once, but I just couldn't clear my mind, you know? I kept thinking about what I was gonna have for dinner. I think we ordered Thai food that night. Pad Thai, extra spicy... \n\nBut, uh, to answer your question, I don't know if it's just the shaving part. I think it's just the razor itself. Just the way it looks when it's open.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel afraid of the straight razor if you were using it for a task that didn't involve shaving?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, I think so. Even if I was just using it to open one of those boxes I was talking about, it wouldn't matter. It's just... I know it’s just a tool, you know? I went to school for this, I trained with it, and I can use clippers and shears all day long without batting an eye. But when that straight razor is just sitting there, open at my station... man, it just feels like I’m about to hurt somebody, or like I'm gonna slip and cut myself really badly. It's crazy because I've never even nicked anyone before. Well, except this one time in barber college, but that was with scissors, not a razor. We were practicing on these mannequin heads first, right? They had real human hair, which was always a little creepy to me. I always wondered where they got the hair from. Like, do people donate it specifically for mannequins, or is it just leftover from wig makers? Anyway, my buddy in class, Steve, he accidentally cut off a chunk of his own thumb while texturizing the mannequin's hair. Bled everywhere. The instructor had to wrap it up in this huge bandage, and Steve couldn't cut hair for a week. He ended up dropping out a month later to go work at his uncle's auto body shop. I saw him a couple of years ago at a gas station, he looked good. He was driving this vintage Mustang that he restored himself. Cherry red, white leather interior... beautiful car. I've always wanted a classic car, maybe a '69 Charger. \n\nBut yeah, anyway, it doesn't matter what I'm using the razor for. Just seeing it open like that, it just... it terrifies me that someone's gonna get hurt.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be afraid of the straight razor if it were blunt and couldn't cut anyone?\n\nPatient: I don't know, maybe? If it was completely blunt and couldn't cut anything... I guess it wouldn't be as bad, but it would still look the same, you know? Just that shape of it. It's funny you mention blunt things, because my mom used to have these butter knives that were so old and worn down they couldn't even cut hot butter. We had this big Sunday breakfast tradition at my house growing up. Pancakes, bacon, the whole nine yards. My dad made the best pancakes. He'd put chocolate chips in them, but only on my brother's side of the griddle, because I hated chocolate chips in my pancakes. I liked blueberries. Fresh ones, not the frozen kind that turn the whole pancake purple. We used to go berry picking every summer at this farm upstate. You'd get these little green baskets and just fill them up. My brother would eat half of what he picked before we even got to the weigh station. The farmer always knew, but he never said anything. He was this old guy with a huge white beard, looked exactly like Santa Claus in overalls. He had a tractor that he let us sit on once. It was a John Deere, bright green. I always thought tractors were so cool when I was a kid. I wanted to be a farmer for like a year, until I realized how early they have to wake up. I am definitely not a morning person. Even now, getting to the barbershop by 8 AM is a struggle. I usually need at least two cups of coffee before I can even hold a conversation. \n\nBut, um, what were we talking about? Oh, the blunt razor. Yeah, I don't know. I think just seeing it open would still give me that panic, even if I knew it was dull.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of the straight razor change if you were using it for a task that is very similar to shaving but not actually shaving, such as shaving cream off a balloon like you did in training?\n\nPatient: Shaving a balloon... man, I haven't thought about that since barber college. We used to go through so many balloons. Just those cheap ones from the dollar store. My instructor, Mr. Davis, he'd blow them up so tight that if you even breathed wrong with the blade, they'd pop and shaving cream would go everywhere. I actually hate the smell of that cheap foam shaving cream now. It reminds me of my grandpa, though. He used to use it every morning. He had this little badger hair brush and a ceramic mug. He was a mechanic, so his hands were always covered in grease, but his face was always perfectly smooth. He worked on airplanes, actually. Commercial jets. We got to go to the hangar once when I was like ten. It's crazy how massive those planes are up close. You don't realize it when you're sitting inside them, eating those tiny bags of pretzels. I always ask for the Biscoff cookies instead, those things are amazing with coffee. I tried to buy a box of them at the grocery store once, but they just didn't taste the same on the ground. Maybe it's the altitude? I read somewhere that your taste buds get numb when you're flying. \n\nBut, um... wait, the balloon thing. Honestly, I'm not really sure if it would change anything. I don't think it would. Because I'd still be holding the razor, and it would still be open in my hand. So yeah, I don't know if what I'm shaving actually makes a difference to the panic.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of the straight razor would decrease if you were using it in a situation where there was no possibility of harming anyone?\n\nPatient: I don't know, honestly. Like if I was alone in a room with it? I guess... I mean, I'd still be holding it, right? And I'd still be the one holding it, so I could still slip and cut myself. My hands get so sweaty when I even think about it. I have to use that talcum powder we have at the shop just to dry them off sometimes. We use this specific brand, Clubman Pinaud. It has that classic barbershop smell, you know? Sort of powdery and masculine. My dad used to wear a cologne that smelled just like it. Old Spice, I think. Or maybe Brut? He always got those gift sets for Christmas with the aftershave and the deodorant. I still buy him one every year, just out of habit. He's retired now, lives down in Florida. Plays golf every single day. I don't get the appeal of golf, honestly. It just seems like a lot of walking in the hot sun to hit a tiny ball. I'd rather be fishing. We used to go fishing on this lake when I was a teenager. Caught a massive largemouth bass once, must have been five pounds. \n\nBut, uh, what were you asking? Oh, if nobody could get hurt. I don't know, I just don't think there's any situation where nobody could get hurt if I'm holding an open razor. I'm there, so I could get hurt. It's just the razor itself, I think.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of the straight razor be less intense if you were to use it for shaving a mannequin or a dummy, rather than a real person?\n\nPatient: A mannequin? Man, I haven't seen one of those since barber college either. We had these heads we called \"pivot points,\" they had this weirdly realistic hair that you could actually cut and style. Some guys would get so attached to their mannequins, they'd name them and talk to them while they were cutting. There was this one guy, Tommy, he named his \"Brenda\" and he'd actually get mad if someone else touched her. Tommy was a trip. He ended up dropping out and moving to Vegas to become a dealer at one of the casinos. Blackjack, I think. I went to Vegas once for my 21st birthday. We stayed at the Luxor, you know, the giant pyramid? It was so confusing inside, the elevators go up at an angle. We got so lost trying to find our room the first night. We ended up at this buffet at like 3 AM eating prime rib and crab legs. The crab legs were okay, but I've had way better in Maryland. My aunt lives in Annapolis, and she makes this crab dip that is just unbelievable. She puts Old Bay seasoning in everything. She even puts it on popcorn, which sounds gross, but it's actually really good if you have a cold beer with it. \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, the dummy. I don't know, honestly. Probably not. Because, like I was saying, it's just the razor itself. It doesn't really matter what's in front of me, real person or plastic head. If I'm holding that blade open, I'm just terrified I'm going to slip and slice my own hand open or something. So no, I don't think it would be any less intense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of the straight razor would decrease if someone else was holding it, but you were still in the room?\n\nPatient: If someone else was holding it... yeah, I think so. If I'm not the one holding it, it's not as bad. Like, the other guys at the shop use them all the time. My buddy Marcus, he's in the chair next to mine, he does these incredibly sharp line-ups with his. He's got this custom-made handle, it's like a dark walnut wood with his initials engraved on it. He paid a fortune for it from this guy on Instagram who makes them by hand. Instagram is crazy for stuff like that now. You can find people making anything. I follow this one account where a guy just restores old axes. He finds them rusted in barns and polishes them up until they look brand new. It's weirdly relaxing to watch. He uses this special grinding wheel, and the sparks fly everywhere. Reminds me of my high school shop class. We had to make a birdhouse, which was so cliché, but I actually tried to make mine look like a miniature log cabin. I spent weeks cutting little dowels for the logs. A squirrel ended up moving into it instead of a bird, though. It chewed the hole bigger and just took over. Squirrels are relentless, man. They get into the bird feeders at my parents' house, and my dad has tried every trick in the book to keep them out. He even put hot sauce on the seeds once, but I think they actually liked it. \n\nBut yeah, anyway, sorry. If Marcus is holding his razor, or anyone else is, I'm fine. I can stand right next to him and talk to him while he's using it. It's just... it's just when it's in *my* hand that I feel like I'm gonna lose control. If I don't have to touch it, the panic isn't really there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of the straight razor is because you feel like you might lose control of it when it's in your hand?\n\nPatient: Yeah, losing control... I think that's exactly it. I mean, I know it’s just a tool I trained with. I can use my clippers all day long without even thinking about it. I actually just upgraded to these new cordless Wahl clippers, and the battery life on them is unbelievable. I used to have the corded ones, and the thick cord would always get tangled around the hydraulic pump on the barber chair. One time, it got so wrapped up I actually tripped and almost took my client down with me. He was a really good sport about it, thankfully. He’s this guy who owns a bakery down the street, and he always brings in these amazing cheese danishes when he comes in for a fade. I don't even have much of a sweet tooth, usually. I'm more of a savory guy, you know? Give me a good steak over a piece of cake any day. But man, those danishes. They remind me of how my mom used to bake when I was a kid. She made this incredible banana bread with chocolate chips in it. We had this massive walnut tree in our backyard, and we'd spend hours in the fall just cracking walnuts on the patio to put in the bread. My hands would get stained completely brown from the husks. It took days to scrub it off. \n\nBut, uh, anyway. Sorry. What were we talking about? Oh, the razor. Yeah, like I was saying, I can use clippers all day, but when that straight razor is just sitting open at my station... it feels like I’m about to hurt somebody or cut myself badly. Just having it sitting there open makes me feel like something terrible is guaranteed to happen.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don't really know if it's one more than the other, honestly. It's just the thought of the blade slipping and doing damage. Like, the idea of a deep cut... My roommate actually sliced his finger open pretty bad just last week chopping onions. He thinks he's this master chef because he watches all those cooking competitions on Netflix. You know the ones where they have to make a gourmet meal out of, like, gummy bears and a can of tuna? He binge-watches those and then tries to do all this rapid-fire knife work in our tiny apartment kitchen. It's a total disaster. The smoke alarm goes off almost every time he cooks. Our landlord actually came banging on the door a few weeks ago because he thought the building was on fire. 

The landlord is this older guy, Mr. Henderson, and he's always wearing these vintage velour tracksuits. Like, full matching sets, burgundy and navy blue, straight out of the 90s. He told us he used to be a breakdancer back in the day, which I just cannot picture at all because he walks with this heavy wooden cane now. He said he blew his knee out doing a backflip off a speaker at a block party in Queens. He's got all these crazy stories about New York in the 80s. 

But, um... sorry, I lost my train of thought again. Hurting myself or others? Yeah, I really couldn't tell you which is worse or if one scares me more. I don't think it's specifically one or the other. It's just this overwhelming feeling that if I'm holding it, a disaster is going to happen, no matter who ends up bleeding.

Some of them go for hundreds of dollars online, which is crazy to me because my grandma used to have the exact same ones and she just kept leftover meatloaf in them. My grandma made the best meatloaf, too. She put a little bit of brown sugar in the ketchup glaze on top. It sounds weird, but it caramelized in the oven and it was incredible. She used to serve it with these mashed potatoes that had way too much butter in them, but nobody complained. \n\nBut, uh, no, the razor thing doesn't really bother me at home or anything. My aunt lives in Annapolis, and she makes this crab dip that is just unbelievable. She puts Old Bay seasoning in everything. Probably not. Because, like I was saying, it's just the razor itself. It doesn't really matter what's in front of me, real person or plastic head. So no, I don't think it would be any less intense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of the straight razor would decrease if someone else was holding it, but you were still in the room?\n\nPatient: If someone else was holding it... yeah, I think so. He paid a fortune for it from this guy on Instagram who makes them by hand. Instagram is crazy for stuff like that now. You can find people making anything. I follow this one account where a guy just restores old axes. He finds them rusted in barns and polishes them up until they look brand new. It's weirdly relaxing to watch. He uses this special grinding wheel, and the sparks fly everywhere. Reminds me of my high school shop class. We had to make a birdhouse, which was so cliché, but I actually tried to make mine look like a miniature log cabin. I spent weeks cutting little dowels for the logs. A squirrel ended up moving into it instead of a bird, though. It chewed the hole bigger and just took over. Squirrels are relentless, man. They get into the bird feeders at my parents' house, and my dad has tried every trick in the book to keep them out. He even put hot sauce on the seeds once, but I think they actually liked it. \n\nBut yeah, anyway, sorry. If Marcus is holding his razor, or anyone else is, I'm fine. I can stand right next to him and talk to him while he's using it. It's just... it's just when it's in *my* hand that I feel like I'm gonna lose control. If I don't have to touch it, the panic isn't really there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of the straight razor is because you feel like you might lose control of it when it's in your hand?\n\nPatient: Yeah, losing control... I'm more of a savory guy, you know? Give me a good steak over a piece of cake any day. But man, those danishes. They remind me of how my mom used to bake when I was a kid. She made this incredible banana bread with chocolate chips in it. We had this massive walnut tree in our backyard, and we'd spend hours in the fall just cracking walnuts on the patio to put in the bread. My hands would get stained completely brown from the husks.
